# Technical Debt

Known issues, compromises, and deferred work. Add an entry any time a problem is identified but not immediately fixed — no exceptions, however small (see [`cadence.md`](cadence.md) §2).

**This file holds open work only.** The summary table below lists exactly the entries that have a body in this file — one row each, no history. When an entry is fully resolved, move the whole body to [`technical_debt_archive.md`](technical_debt_archive.md), delete its summary row, and replace **Fix** with the resolution date plus a pointer to whichever doc/code now carries the lasting content. An entry that is only *partly* resolved stays here, with what shipped and what remains spelled out in its **Status**.

IDs are `TD-` plus a zero-padded three-digit number, assigned in order and never reused. Priority is a field, never part of the ID.

Two header lines follow **Added:** so a worker can filter the file instead of re-reading it (TD-118): **Owner** — who moves the entry next (`anchor`, `designer`, `grinder`, `paul`, `dev-cadence`); **Kind** — what the next step is (`build`, `design-first`, `live-check`, `evaluation`, `decision`). Every open entry carries them; keep them true when the Status changes. **Pickable is derived, never written** (cadence §2.4, TD-228): an entry is pickable while its `**Blocked by:**` names nothing still open and no decision, so what waits on another entry or on a person's word says so there, with its pointer; what nothing blocks and a grinder still cannot build — a live look, an evaluation, the anchor's edit — is said by its **Kind** or its **Owner**, and the Status says what is next. A `live-check` is followed on its line by its build's last merged PR — `**Kind:** live-check #<n>`, written by the PR that makes the entry a live check — and once that PR is live (`ao promote status`) the check is in a `free-pick` lane whose owner it has (design §4.9b, TD-320); cadence's script reads the word alone. A grinder's pick list is therefore `ao repo` (its pickable entries, a live check whose build is live among them), and `python3 scripts/ledger.py --list --pickable yes --owner grinder --kind build` lists the builds alone; the designer's is `--owner designer --kind design-first`. The line below is the one list of the **Owner** and **Kind** words (cadence §2.12): `scripts/ledger.py` flags a value outside it, and `tests/test_ledger.py` reads its sets from it and checks the two lines' shape, so a new word is added there and nowhere else.

Fields: Owner = anchor | designer | grinder | paul | dev-cadence; Kind = build | design-first | live-check | evaluation | decision

## TD-003: Phone layout: narrow Focus with a soft-key row

**Priority:** Medium
**Type:** feature
**Added:** 2026-09-06
**Owner:** paul
**Kind:** build
**Status:** **Paul, 2026-10-07:** reserved until the real connection path (how the phone reaches the home) is built. Open **Next:** Paul's (the grinder brief reserves it).
**Location:** `src/agentorc/ui/static/app.css` (`@media (max-width: 720px)`), `focus.html`

**Why:** Design §4.5 (phone, phase 2): Team collapses to cards with Allow / Deny, Focus gets a narrow mode with the terminal full-width and a soft-key row (↑ ↓ ← → Enter Esc Tab 1–9) so questions are still answered through the terminal. The CSS has a bare media query; there is no soft-key row and nothing has been tried on a phone.

**Fix:** soft-key row that sends keys through the terminal websocket (not `send-keys`, invariant 6), collapsed side panel, 44 px tap targets on Allow / Deny; test on a phone over WireGuard or the Cloudflare tunnel (design §4.5) once phase 2 lands it.

**Related:** design §10 "Phone answers for questions" (open).

## TD-005: `pretrust()` can lose a concurrent Claude Code rewrite of `.claude.json`

**Priority:** Low
**Added:** 2026-09-06
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** evaluation
**Status:** Open **Next:** needs an attended `claude` run.
**Location:** `src/agentorc/adapters/claude_code/__init__.py` (`pretrust`)

**Why:** The first-run trust quirk does a read-modify-write of Claude Code's own `.claude.json` under an flock that only agentorc takes. A running Claude Code session that rewrites the file inside that window loses its write (or we lose our flag, which merely re-shows the dialog). Raised in the PR #3 review; the window is one read plus one write at launch time.

**Fix:** either watch for the dialog in the pane as the fallback and answer it through the terminal channel, or find a supported way to pre-trust a directory (a `--trust`-style flag or a per-project settings key) and drop the file edit. Done when no agentorc code writes `.claude.json`.

**Related:** TD-006.

## TD-006: `.claude.json` location under a custom `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is assumed, not verified

**Priority:** Low
**Added:** 2026-09-06
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** evaluation
**Status:** Open **Next:** needs an attended `claude` run.
**Location:** `src/agentorc/adapters/claude_code/__init__.py` (`global_config_file`)

**Why:** For a profile with `config_dir` set, the adapter reads/writes `<config_dir>/.claude.json`. The docs say every `~/.claude` path moves under `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, but `~/.claude.json` is not under `~/.claude`, and no second-account profile exists yet to test it. If wrong, pretrust silently writes a file Claude Code never reads and the trust dialog appears for that profile.

**Fix:** create a throwaway `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, run `claude` once, see where `.claude.json` lands, pin it with a test. Done when the second profile (grind) launches without the dialog.

**Related:** TD-005, design §4.2a.

## TD-026: Scheduling: start/stop times and window overrides for unattended sessions, editable from the UI

**Priority:** Medium
**Type:** feature
**Added:** 2026-09-10
**Owner:** grinder
**Kind:** build
**Status:** Partly done — **round 2, 2026-09-25 (the designer, PR #548), on Paul's answer to the scope ask (*keep start_at and a scheduled state for one session as well*):** §6 *Start time* (`start_at`; the record created now with its launch record and the directory's slot, the `scheduled` state, the session created at the instant by the tick, `ao at`, Cancel), §4.2's state, §4.5a's *starts* note, **At** field and ◷ glyph, §4.7's verbs, the glossary's *start time*; the build is **TD-152**. Dropped on the same answer: window overrides with an expiry (TD-101), calendar-shaped windows, one-off runs. Was: **design round 2026-09-24 (the designer, PR #526):** the start at the reset is designed as §6 *Schedule* — a replay of the team's last person-pressed start from its launch records at the `resets` of a named profile's window, kept in `settings.yml` `schedules:` (§5), set by `ao schedule` (§4.7), shown as the team card's *starts* note (§4.5a); the build is **TD-133**, unscheduled until Paul says (2026-09-22: *not vital, not scheduled*). **A team-wide stop time** was designed 2026-09-25 as a setting (§6 *Team stop time*, `teams.<team>.until`, the Settings page and `ao team until`; TD-146 builds it), which answers the *editing a stop time from the page* shape for a team. The other shapes below — `start_at` and a `scheduled` state for one session, window overrides with an expiry (TD-101 already says *not wanted yet*), calendar-shaped windows, one-off runs — are a scope question put to Paul the same day (ask m-32d769985798; the recommendation: build the reset start, drop the rest, and let the phase-3 run window be its own design when it comes). Was: the per-session **stop time** landed 2026-09-13 (PR #129): `run_until` on the record, `ao new --unattended --until 06:00|+8h|<ISO>`, `ao until <session> <when>|--clear`, the wrap-up-then-kill on the tick, and *stops 06:00* on `ao status -v`. That closes gap (1) below — a hand-started worker now has a stopper. Open: `start_at` and the `scheduled` state, window overrides with an expiry, calendar-shaped schedules, one-off runs, and the UI *beyond* the stop time — the card and Focus header say *stops 06:00* and the New session form has an **Until** field (PR #131), **and the Focus header's badge became the control that edits it on 2026-09-14 (PR #147)** — click for a time, empty to clear, drawn only for an unattended session since the agent refuses one on an interactive session either way; a session with no stop time shows a dim *no stop time* rather than nothing, because "nothing will stop this" is the fact a person opening Focus most needs. The review of that PR found a live bug the control would have made easy to hit: `set_stop` cleared `wrapup_sent_at` on every call, so re-confirming an unchanged, already-elapsed stop time asked a session inside its wrap-up grace to wrap up a second time — and, since the ask happens before the grace check, repeatedly touching the control deferred the forced kill indefinitely, which is this entry's own gap (1) reintroduced through the edit path. Only a *different* time now counts as a new run. Latent and not reached today: every caller passes the one shared `WRAPUP_PROMPT`, so the control cannot yet clobber a custom wrap-up text, but it would if custom texts were ever exposed. The card shows no next transition for a repo window because there is no repo window. The repo window is still phase 3; when it lands it should *set* a stop time rather than be a second mechanism. **2026-09-22:** one more schedule shape, decided by Paul the same day: **a team start at the usage window's reset**, using the reset time the adapter already reports (§4.3, TD-073) — configurable, **off by default**, set from the settings page TD-100 describes; not vital, not scheduled. **Next:** designed; the builds are TD-133 (the reset start, unscheduled until Paul says) and TD-152 (the start time); this entry archives with them.
**Blocked by:** TD-133, TD-152
**Location:** design §6 (policies), §4.5a (New session, card / Focus header), §5 `.agentorc.yml` `unattended:` block

**Why:** Design §6 gives unattended sessions one schedule: the repo's weekly `window` (phase 3, the tdgrind port). That covers the nightly grind and nothing else. Two gaps showed on 2026-09-10, the first day the three samscrape tdgrind workers ran under agentorc instead of the cron supervisor: (1) a worker started by hand with `ao new --unattended` outside the window has **no stopper at all** — nothing wraps it up at 06:00, at a usage cap, or when the token lapses, so "start it now so I can watch it" means "remember to close it yourself"; (2) every deviation from the weekly window is a hand edit to a config file — tdgrind's `~/.tdgrind/config` carries dated shell one-liners (`[ "$(date -u +%s)" -lt <epoch> ] && WINDOW_START=0 …`) for "Paul is out today, run all day", which is a schedule expressed as code, invisible to the Team and forgotten once it lapses. A scheduler is the general form of both: a session or repo carries *when it may run*, the policy tick enforces it, and the UI shows and edits it.

**Fix:** treat the weekly window as one kind of schedule and add the others on the same policy tick, all visible on the card:

- **Per-session `run_until` / `start_at`** on the New session form and the `ao new` flags (`--until 06:00`, `--until +8h`, `--at 20:00`): a stop time gets wrap-up-then-kill exactly like leaving the window; a start time queues the session (a new `scheduled` state on the Org page, with the time) and the tick launches it. A session started by hand with `--unattended` and no `--until` inherits the repo window's next close, so gap (1) cannot recur.
  **`--until` landed 2026-09-13 (PR #129)**: the CLI parses `06:00` (the next one, in the caller's local clock), `+8h` and ISO and hands the agent an absolute instant — "the next 06:00" is a question about the caller's clock, so the record never has to answer it. At the instant the agent sends the wrap-up prompt once and kills the session when it settles or ten minutes later; the wording travels on the record (like `ledger`) because `sessionorc` must not know what a brief is, and it is the same text `ao team stop` sends. `ao until <session> <when>|--clear` sets or clears it afterwards, through a `set_stop` RPC gated as an acting call — a stop time is a `kill` with a delay on it. `--until` without `--unattended` is refused rather than stored: a stop time nothing will act on is this entry's own failure inverted. The UI followed in PR #131: the **stops** note on the card and the Focus header (§4.5a), from the same formatter `ao status -v` uses, and the New session **Until** field, which refuses a stop time on a session that is not unattended exactly as `ao new` does. Left here: `start_at`, the `scheduled` state, editing a stop time from the page (the CLI's `ao until` has no page equivalent yet), and the inheritance from the repo window, which needs the window (phase 3) first.
- **Window overrides with an expiry**, per repo, kept in agent state rather than the checked-in `.agentorc.yml`: "run all day until 2026-09-02 20:00", "pause until Monday" (the PAUSE flag with a date), caps to 99% until the weekly reset. Shown on the repo's row with the expiry; expired overrides fall away by themselves. This replaces the dated one-liners in tdgrind's config.
- **Calendar-shaped schedules** in the `unattended:` block beyond weekday/weekend: a list of `{days, hours}` rules, and a `tz` (the window is in the host's local time today, which is right on one host and ambiguous on two — phase 2 adds the second host).
- **One-off runs**: schedule a *command* session (§4.5 Commands) or a brief at a time — "run the deploy at 02:00", "start a review worker Friday evening" — which is the Commands tab plus a time. This is the dev-cadence `/schedule`-style routine, local and tmux-backed.
- Edits from the UI go through agent RPCs (`set_schedule`, `set_override`), never by writing the repo file; the checked-in block is the default, overrides layer on top. `ao status` shows the next transition ("stops 06:00", "starts Fri 20:00") per session and per repo.

Done when: a hand-started unattended session shows when it will stop and stops then; "run all day today" is one click or one `ao` line with an expiry, not a config edit; the Team shows a scheduled-but-not-started session; and tdgrind's `config` overrides file has nothing left to express.

**Related:** design §6 run window / usage gate / PAUSE, §7 phase 3 (tdgrind migration — this is the step after it, or the shape the port should take), TD-010 (adopting hand-started sessions faces the same "who stops it" question), samscrape TD-274 (the tdgrind supervisor), samscrape `~/.tdgrind/config` dated overrides of 2026-09-01.

## TD-035: Adapters without a session-start hook still run dev-cadence's SessionStart set

**Priority:** Low
**Added:** 2026-09-12
**Owner:** grinder
**Kind:** build
**Status:** Open — **designed 2026-09-24 (the designer, PR #530):** the 2026-09-12 decision is written into design §4.3 *A repo's start hooks are every adapter's to run* (the tool's start hook, else the runner at launch with its stdout prepended to the first prompt; a hand-started session untouched; the anchor check's source for another tool decided with that adapter). The build is the second adapter's, whichever TD-112's spike picks; this entry is its checklist and archives with it. Was: design question; no second adapter exists yet **Next:** nothing to build until a second adapter exists (TD-112's spike); the rule is written.
**Blocked by:** TD-112
**Location:** `src/agentorc/adapters/<tool>/` (a future Gemini CLI / Codex CLI adapter), `src/agentorc/adapters/claude_code/__init__.py` (`CADENCE_HOOK_LINE`, the pattern to generalise), design §4.2, §4.3

**Why:** dev-cadence's per-repo conventions are tool-neutral in their rules and scripts (cadence.md, the ledger and board, `check_cadence.py`, `cadence_hooks.sh`, the pre-push hook, the review-evidence PR comment) but their *wiring* is Claude Code's: the runner line lives in `.claude/settings.json` and in this adapter's launch layer, the runner resolves the repo from `CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR`, and `check_anchor.py` reads the live-session registry under `~/.claude/sessions`. A repo worked by another tool gets the rules and none of the delivery: no attention board at session start, no cadence-changes entry, no anchor warning. Decided 2026-09-12 (with Paul): tool-specific wiring belongs in the harness adapter, not in the repo and not with a per-repo agent — the repo does not know which tool will work it; the harness does.

**Fix:** (1) every adapter's launch runs `scripts/cadence_hooks.sh --session-start` for the session directory when the script is executable there: through the tool's own start hook where it has one (Gemini CLI hooks, design §4.3 table), otherwise by running the runner at launch with the hook payload it would have received and prepending its stdout to the first prompt — non-intrusive, nothing needed from the repo; (2) the runner takes the repo root from an argument or `PWD` as well as `CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR` (dev-cadence change; it already falls back to `git rev-parse --show-toplevel`) — settled by design §4.3 and ADR 2026-10-08 (TD-159): the runner falls back to `pwd`, and nothing is owed to dev-cadence; (3) the anchor check needs an equivalent of the live-session registry for the other tool, or agentorc's own session records as the source when the session was launched by it — decide when the second adapter lands. Done when a session launched through a second adapter in a dev-cadence consumer prints the due-items line and the unseen cadence-changes entries at its start, and a hand-started session of that tool is untouched.

**Related:** design §4.2 (the launch layer carries the line, 2026-09-11), §4.3 (adapter contract, the hooks table), §4.8 (relay); dev-cadence cadence §3 (one SessionStart line; who makes the change, 2026-09-12); TD-028 (orchestrator brief).

## TD-036: Orchestrator membership: `controllers` on the target, the gate's second half, `set_controllers`, and the surface

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-12
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** build
**Status:** **The director's round ending done 2026-10-08 (the anchor, PR #1289):** `docs/briefs/director.md`'s round ends in `ao wait --timeout 3540` with the manager brief's fallback (`loop`, `ScheduleWakeup` 3600 s) and its context-bound ending, logs a round line, and its *exited* step now leaves the restart to the tick's rule 1 and reads `restarts` / `restart_ceiling`, as design §4.8 *A director is not a special case* says (the brief still had the director restarting a member itself); it says *manager* for its members where it said *lead*. **Left: step (6) alone**, the migration, which `ao team start` (TD-040) superseded — Paul's to run or declare stale; asked of the techlead as a steer. **Triaged 2026-10-06 (the anchor): stays the anchor's.** Step (6), the migration (`docs/briefs/archive/td036-migration.md`), is Paul's to run, or to declare stale, so no member can close the entry. Open — design written 2026-09-12 (§4.8, §4.5a, §9 invariant 11, §10, [ADR](decisions/2026-09-12-orchestrator-membership-prior-art.md)); **go given by Paul 2026-09-13**, implementation handed to session `orc-membership-design` in the six steps below, the UI step after the Herd → Team rename lands. Step (1) landed 2026-09-13 (the `controllers` field, the gate's membership half, create-adds-creator with attenuated grants, the `set_controllers` RPC, tests); step (2) landed 2026-09-13 (`ao control`, `ao new --controller`, both directions in `ao status -v`, the no-controller line, the skill text); step (3) landed 2026-09-13 (the card chip, the Focus controllers chip, the orchestrator Members list, the New session picker); step (5) landed 2026-09-13 (the orchestrator brief's membership scope and restart ceiling, the two new briefs, the launch-order note in the briefs README, the `guardians` facts and the devcontainer question in §10); step (6) is **written and waiting on Paul** — `docs/briefs/archive/td036-migration.md` has the fleet, the commands and the verification, but running it needs the installed venv at `~/.local/share/agentorc-venv` upgraded (its `ao` has no `control` subcommand) and `agentorc-agent` restarted under five live unattended workers, which is Paul's to do, not a session's (board item 2026-09-13); step (4) landed 2026-09-13 (PR #116, as TD-040 step (a)): `agentorc.repoconfig` reads `.agentorc.yml`, `ao new` without `--controller` takes the preset's `controllers:`, else the repo's, resolving names to ids in the session's directory (a configured name that is not running is skipped with one stderr line naming it and the file, never an error, so a stale default cannot block every start in the repo; an explicit `--controller` naming an unknown session still refuses, since the person typed it; a preset's `controllers: []` means *nobody*, deliberately, and does not fall back to the repo's list), the New session picker is ticked from the same rule, and the no-controller line still prints when the result is empty. Execution order differs from the numbering below: Paul's instruction was (1), (2), (4), then (3) once rebased onto the Herd→Team rename, then (5) and (6); (4) was blocked on the loader when it was reached and followed once TD-040 built it, so what landed is (1), (2), (3), (5), (4), with (6) written but not run (and superseded by `ao team start`, TD-040). The director's missing round ending (from TD-049's archive, 2026-09-20) is the 2026-10-08 line above. **Next:** step (6) declared stale or run, then archive.
**Location:** `src/sessionorc/agent.py` (`_gate`, the session record, `create`, a new `set_controllers` RPC), `src/agentorc/cli.py` (`ao new --controller`, `ao control`, `ao status -v`, the skill text), `src/agentorc/ui/` (card chip, Focus controllers chip, orchestrator Members list, New session picker), the `.agentorc.yml` loader (`controllers:`)

**Why:** `orchestrate` is one bit on the *caller* and `_gate` never looks at the target, so any session holding the grant may act on every session on the host. That held while there was one orchestrator; several are in sight at once (`guardians`, a ui orc and a backend orc in one large repo, a read-only status orc, an orchestrator-of-orchestrators), and each would reach every session. Per-repo boundaries were rejected: the person says explicitly which sessions each orchestrator controls. The membership lives on the *target* so the gate is one lookup, nothing has to be kept in step, it survives a restart and dies with the record. The prior-art survey added the pieces the design lacked — a bounded restart ceiling, `one_for_one` scope, no propagation of an orchestrator's exit to its workers, explicit re-attachment rather than automatic reparenting.

**Fix:** in order, each its own PR: (1) `controllers: [session ids]` on the session record, persisted; `_gate` passes an acting RPC from a session only when the caller holds `orchestrate` **and** is in the target's list (empty = nobody), both read live per call, with tests for the refusal, the several-controllers case, the empty default, and that a person (no caller) is unaffected; `create` adds the creator and refuses grants the creator does not hold; a `set_controllers` RPC gated on the target like `set_grants`. (2) CLI: `ao new --controller <id>…`, `ao control <orc> add|remove <session>…`, both directions in `ao status -v`, and the `ao --skill` text gains the membership half of the gate. (3) UI: the card's *under `<orc>`* chip, the Focus controllers chip, the orchestrator's Members list, the New session controller picker (§4.5a). (4) `.agentorc.yml` `controllers:` per repo and per preset (§5), and the one line `ao new` prints when a session starts with no controller. (5) briefs: orchestrator-ao-1 run 3, the `guardians` orchestrator, and the orc-of-orcs — each stating its members, the restart ceiling (N per period, then escalate to the board), `one_for_one` scope, and "supervisors only supervise". (6) migration: the five running workers have no list, so promoting orchestrator-ao-1 must attach it to them (`ao control orchestrator-ao-1 add …`) **in the same step**, or the promotion silently takes its reach away. Done when two orchestrators run on one host, each acts only on its own members, `ao kill` across the boundary is refused by the gate rather than by a brief, and a worker card says who is over it.

**Related:** design §4.8, §4.5a, §5, §9 invariant 11, §10 (2026-09-12); [ADR 2026-09-12](decisions/2026-09-12-orchestrator-membership-prior-art.md); TD-028 (the grant this builds on), TD-026.

## TD-052: Messages between sessions — build design §4.10

**Priority:** High
**Added:** 2026-09-14 (raised by Paul)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** live-check #343
**Status:** Open — **the design landed first, as §4.10 (2026-09-14)**, with three rows in §4.5a, a sentence in §4.7, invariants 5 and 11 amended and invariant 13 added, and the §10 question answered; **extended 2026-09-16** with how a Claude Code session is told it has mail (the doorbell, the `Stop` hook, the line on every `ao` reply) and the lifecycle of an entry; **revised twice 2026-09-16** after a Fable review of all of §4.10 (thirteen changes, §10's entry of that date — among them the wake budget's refill rule, the person inbox, copies to other controllers, the `Stop`-hook block cut, and the build order below — then sixteen more from a second Fable review: the host agent decides mail wakes when a recipient is reachable, `wait` moves into the host agent, exact thread counting, pending asks on exit; **a fourth Fable review the same day** adopted ten more — open asks never pruned, the pair tally windowed, a non-wake leaves the watermark, repeats refused not dropped, `sends` recorded on the target for `conflict`, the controller's `send` charge dropped, the skill file displaces, a person's Message control, wall-clock ask bounds, person-inbox refusals name the board — and Sonnet review rounds followed, §10). **Step 1 landed 2026-09-17 (PR #163, Fable)**: `sessionorc.mail` holds both gates as functions over a record map (`act_gate` is the old `_gate` moved; `message_gate` reads the four edges) and the bound constants, every one a placeholder until step 6 (`None` = unlimited; each refusal path is built and tested by monkeypatching it); `MailEntry`, `Tally` and `SendEntry` on the record (`inbox`, `outbox`, `threads`, `sends`, `superseded_by`, `host`), with `view()` — what `list`, `get` and the push carry — dropping bodies for `unread` and `mail` marks; the `msg` and `inbox` RPCs; `sends` written by `send`/`keys` at the gate with the submit's verdict, and `ao status -v` printing the last three plus the unread count and marks; the tick's `_sweep_mail` (expiry, pending on exit, expired on close, retention); `_supersede` moving mail and rewriting ids; a same-nonce retry returning the first verdict. Two things the step text did not say, decided in the code and written into §4.10: the sender keeps its own copy of every entry (`outbox`) so the sender-side marks are on its record, and `ACTING_RPCS` moved to `mail.py` beside the gates (re-exported from `agent.py`). Two things left to later steps deliberately: `ao msg` / `ao inbox` as CLI commands and the person inbox (step 2 — a `to` of `person` is refused naming the board until then); the identical-repeat and per-sender damping (step 6, with the numbers). `tests/test_mail.py` carries the tests, one per rule. **Step 8 landed 2026-09-16 (PR #165, Opus)** for the four session-side rows of §4.5a — the Focus Inbox panel, the card's unread chip, Reply, and Message — plus an `inbox_delete` RPC (a person's only, one record's copy); the Org top bar's person inbox followed once step 2 had merged: **step 8 completed 2026-09-16 (PR #168, Opus)** — the top bar's count (rendered on the Org page, then polled from the `inbox` RPC: nothing pushed carries it), its panel with Reply and delete, and `inbox_delete` naming no session deleting from the person inbox; no Reply on an entry the person sent, by design — a person does not answer themselves, and the session's answer lands in the person inbox. **Step 2 landed 2026-09-16 (PR #166, Opus)**: `ao msg`, `ao inbox` (the fixed header, each entry marked controller / person / other) and the person inbox in `person_inbox.json` beside `sessions/`, with the mail rules in `ao --skill`; the top bar reads it with the `inbox` RPC given no caller and no `id`, and replies with `msg` given `reply_to` and no `to`. Decisions the step text did not make: the person inbox keeps no exchange tally — only the sending session's record counts, and `PERSON_INBOX_DEPTH` / `PERSON_SENDER_DEPTH` bound the rest (a sentence in §4.10); a session's `reply` to a person's message with no addressee lands in the person inbox, and a person may not write to it; its depths count unread entries, which — a person's read setting nothing — is every entry until a person-inbox delete exists (`inbox_delete` covered session inboxes only until PR #168 added the person inbox); every session sender shows as `[other]` there, since no session is the person's controller; `ao msg --reply-to <id> "…"` may omit the addressee and defaults `--kind` to `reply`; `ao inbox` at a terminal reads only the person inbox; and to hold 120 lines the skill's membership and verify-every-send paragraphs were tightened as well as the `ao wait` one. **Step 4 landed 2026-09-16 (PR #170, Opus)**, text only: the mail rules in `src/agentorc/briefs/orchestrator.md`, `grinder.md`, `hunter.md`, `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.md` and `docs/briefs/tdgrind-ao-1.md` — a lead's round reads `ao inbox --unread` first and answers every `ask`; a grinder reads its inbox before claiming, notes its same-team siblings at claim and mails its lead at `done`; `ao msg person` for what is not yet a board item; the controllers-and-people rule once per brief; and an *unknown method* fallback to `ao progress` / `ao finding` for a host agent older than the build. The live leads' ticks still run on `loop` rather than ending in `ao wait` (TD-049 steps 5–6), and `ao wait` returns on mail only once step 3 lands; the briefs take effect on the next `ao team start`. **Step 3 landed 2026-09-16 (PR #171, Opus)**: `wait` is the host agent's RPC (`ao wait` a thin call, which says in one line and exits 1 against an agent that predates it), held on its own connection and cancelled when that connection closes; the wake decision is `_decide_wake`, the one function step 7's doorbell calls; `mail_decided`, `wakes` and `wake_refilled_at` are home-owned fields on the record; `WAKE_BUDGET` (None), `WAKE_WINDOW` and `WAKES_KEEP` sit in `mail.py`; every response to a session with unread mail carries `mail` in its envelope, and the CLI prints the line after every command that reached the agent. Decisions the step text did not make: mail's half of the per-caller cursor *is* the `mail_decided` watermark, so no mail is kept in the `waits/` file; a first wait wakes on no member's change but does decide unread mail already waiting; a wait returns mail as headers only (id, from, role, kind, about, at), so `read_at` stays `ao inbox`'s; `ao --json wait` prints `{changed, mail, wake}` rather than a bare list; a person's session blocked in `wait` is never returned by mail (invariant 5); a person's `keys` refills like a `send`, and a person's message refills its named addressees, not its copies; the watermark, `wakes` and the refill move with a resume; the unread line goes to stderr under `--json`, and a command that never reached the agent (`ao --skill`) prints none; a watermark whose entry has left the inbox falls back to its `at`, compared `>=` (a redundant wake, never a missed one). Step 5 reads each record's `wakes`: `cause: mail, charged: true` are mail-caused wakes, `cause: member, charged: false` the free ones, `covered` the entries each took in. The briefs' *once step 3 lands* caveat stays true until the live agent is restarted onto this code. Steps 5, 6 and 7 below stay open. **The workers building them run from briefs in `docs/briefs/archive/td052-step*.md`** (steps 2 and 8: PR #164; steps 3, 4 and the top-bar row 8b: PR #167 — both merged on a failing `ledger` row, which this sentence repairs: a brief names the step it builds, and the ledger is where that is recorded), one worker per step under orchestrator-ao-1, each step its own PR. Code steps below, each its own PR, in order **Found watching two live teams, 2026-09-17:** delivery, three of the four gate edges in use (upward, downward, team; no *shared target* yet), the pair tallies and the person's notes all behave as §4.10 says, but **no record has a single `wakes` entry**: both leads pace their rounds with the `loop` timer and never call `ao wait`, because the lead brief says only that `ao wait` *returns on new mail* and never tells the lead to block in it between rounds. So mail to a lead waits for its next 10-minute round, and step 5 has no wake data to measure. Before step 5 can produce numbers the lead brief needs the sentence (block in `ao wait --timeout <round>` between rounds), which takes effect at the next team start. **Done 2026-09-17 (PR #194):** the lead template and `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.md` end every round in `ao wait --timeout 540`, with the `loop` timer kept only as the fallback when `ao wait` itself fails, and a quiet round no longer costs a commit. Not seen live yet. **Step 6 landed 2026-09-18 (PR #214):** every placeholder set from step 5's measurement (eight hours of samscrape-grind, 2026-09-17), with the evidence and the rule *twice the measured traffic stays clear* written into §4.10 *The numbers*: thread 40, pair 300 per 24 h (`PAIR_BOUND`, split from the thread bound — one number could not serve both), mailbox 100 unread, person inbox 200 / 20 per sender, retention 12 h, wake budget 30 an hour. A thread's tally is pruned with its last entry, and the bookkeeping fields ride only a `get`. Step 5 is what it was — the records of any night's team. **Step 7 landed 2026-09-20 (PR #343, Opus, grinder-ao-1):** the doorbell — each tick, a hook-confirmed `idle` session with unread mail no wake has covered and an empty composer is rung with `sessionorc.mail.unread_line`, the one text the per-command line prints, as its own task; the ring is `_decide_wake` (`via: doorbell` on the wake), so the budget and *rung only for new mail* are the watermark's; one ring per idle stretch; a person's session, a pane with no `composer()`, a session blocked in `wait` and a wrap-up under way are skipped. *A wrap-up under way* gained `wrapup_at`, stamped by a `send` marked `wrapup` (Wrap up and `ao team stop`, the manager's wind-down) and cleared by the next plain send, because the host agent cannot tell the wrap-up prompt from another send by its text. A failed ring is retried once without a second charge and only into an empty composer, then written as `doorbell_failed`, which `ao status -v` prints. As built in design §4.10 after the doorbell's rules. **What is left of this entry:** the second-adapter step below (with TD-035) and the *Done when*'s live half — seen on the running system after a promote (docs/user_attention.md). **Next:** the second-adapter step waits on TD-112, the live half on a promote.
**Blocked by:** TD-112

**Location:** `src/sessionorc/models.py` (the `inbox` on the record), `src/sessionorc/agent.py` (`rpc_msg`, `rpc_inbox`, a new `rpc_wait` — `ao wait` today runs wholly in `src/agentorc/cli.py` (`cmd_wait`, `wait_scope`, `wake_changes`) and moves here — the message gate beside `_gate`, `_supersede`), `src/sessionorc/store.py` (persistence with the record), `src/agentorc/cli.py` (`ao msg`, `ao inbox`, `ao wait`), `src/agentorc/ui/` (the Inbox panel, the unread chip, Reply), `src/agentorc/skill.md`, design §4.10 / §4.5a / §4.7 / §9 invariants 5, 11, 13

**Why:** four kinds of session-to-session traffic exist — lead → worker, worker → lead, lead ↔ lead, worker ↔ worker — and one mechanism served all of them: `ao send`, the host agent typing keystrokes into a pane. Only the first works. A worker reaches upward through `progress` and `findings`, which are the wrong shape for it: declarations about references, addressed to nobody and delivered to nobody. They are ungated — any session may write any record's — but that is not a channel: writing onto another session's record states a fact about it rather than telling it anything, and nothing carries it to the session that must read it; two leads are peers, so TD-036's gate refuses them each other; two workers coordinate by side effects — a ledger row, a branch name, a PR that already claims the reference. Paul, 2026-09-14: *"we need to make sure agent to agent comms are first class in our design."* The cause is a conflation, not four missing features: the `control` grant (`orchestrate` until TD-055) + `controllers` answers *may A act on B?*, and messaging was folded into it because keystrokes were the only delivery there was — typing into a pane genuinely is an act of control. §4.10 splits the two. This entry builds it.

**Fix, in order — each step is usable on its own.** Reordered 2026-09-16 after the Fable review of §4.10: everything that types nothing comes first, a team runs on it, and the wake budget is set from what that team spent *before* the doorbell — the only part that types into a pane — is built.

1. **The record and the RPCs.** Built with TD-057 step 1's address choices from the start — `host` on the record, ids qualified on the way in, message ids, `at` and nonces minted by the host agent, the gates as functions over a record map — so a second host needs no migration. `inbox` on the session record (persisted and reloaded with it, dies when it is forgotten); the `msg` and `inbox` RPCs; the message gate as its own function beside `_gate`, refusing by naming §4.10's graph rather than invariant 11, with a test per edge — upward, downward, same `team` badge (invariant 9's named exception), shared controlled target — and per refusal. The interactive split is its own test: an acting RPC onto a person's session still refused, a message to it landing and never waking (invariant 5). Also in this step, because each is a field or a rule on the entry: `read_at` set only when `inbox` returns the entry to its caller (never by `wait`, never by a person's UI read); `text` capped; **threads** — a `reply` belongs to its root's thread whatever its `about`, messages replying to nothing count per pair, and the tally is a field on **both** participants' records, never recounted from surviving entries; the **automatic copy** of a controller's mail `about` a session to that session's other controllers, expanded into `to` at send time and **exempt** from the recipient cap and from all-or-nothing (a copy that cannot land is dropped and recorded as `copies_failed` on the sender's entry), with replies in a copied thread copied to the same set; the recipient cap counting only sender-named addressees, and a multi-addressee send **all or nothing**, refused naming the failing addressee; the **thread tally per root on every record holding an entry of it** (the reply-less pair tally within a rolling window, never lifetime), counting `ask`/`note`/`conflict` (the first `reply` to an open `ask` never counted, never refused, and closing it; later replies, and replies to a `note`, counted as notes; `reply_to` required to name an entry in the replier's own inbox), checked against the sender's and each named addressee's tally, never a copy recipient's; an `ask`'s bound (wall-clock, home's clock) running from `at`, read or not, and an open `ask` or `conflict` exempt from pruning until it closes or expires; a bounded **`sends`** list on every record — `{id, from, at, text}` for each `send`/`keys` that reached it, written at the gate — printed by `ao status` and cited by id in a `conflict` — which is an `ask` for every rule (bound, first reply free, pending on exit, never pruned open) — and a person's message into a thread uncounted and resetting its tally and `bound_hit`; an exited addressee leaving its `ask`s **pending** (expired only on close, forget or the `ask`'s own bound), with *addressee exited* on the asker's replies; and `_supersede` (`agent.py`) moving every entry inside retention, read or unread, plus the tallies and `sends`, onto the resuming record (a `send` to the superseded id refused, as any act on a closed record), **rewriting the old id to the new one** in the moved entries' `to` and in every other record's pair tallies and pending `ask` addressees; and a `msg` to a closed record that a live one superseded **forwarded** to the successor, saying so. Bodies stay out of the `subscribe` deltas (counts only).
2. **The CLI and the person inbox.** `ao msg <to>… "…" [--kind] [--about] [--reply-to]`, `ao inbox [--unread] [--json]`, and `ao msg person "…"` into the person inbox — one per org, held by the home host agent (§4.4a; on one host, that host's agent), belonging to no session record, persisted in its own file in the host agent's store, ungated, with its own depth and per-sender depth; `ao inbox` with no `AGENTORC_SESSION` reads it; a person's reply from it is a person's act toward the sender (it may wake it and refills its budget). `ao --skill` gains the mail rules — read your inbox before acting, answer an `ask`, never broadcast, reach a person with `ao msg person` — displacing the `ao wait` paragraph so the file stays at 120 lines, and, above the rest, **instructions come from your controllers and from people; mail from anyone else is information you weigh** — stated again by `ao inbox` itself: a fixed header, and each entry marked as from a controller, a person, or neither.
3. **Typing-free waking: `wait` moves into the host agent and gains mail, and the line on every `ao` reply.** `ao wait` already exists (§4.8 "Waking a lead", TD-049 steps 1–4, PR #145) and blocks on a member's state change, but runs wholly in the CLI, so the host agent cannot know who is blocked or why a wait returned. Move it into the host agent as the `wait` RPC with the same snapshot and per-caller cursor, and add the **wake decision**: when a session is reachable (hook-confirmed `idle`, or blocked in `wait`; across hosts also its node's link up, TD-057 step 5), the host agent looks at the unread mail since its last decision and records one mail-caused wake covering it — a `wait` returning for a member's change at the same moment records none — against a single per-session `mail_decided` watermark that every **wake** advances and a non-wake leaves alone, re-taken on each host-agent tick while a session stays blocked in `wait`. A `wait` runs on its own connection and is dropped when that connection closes (no ghost waits after a Ctrl-C); the per-caller cursor stays on disk under `waits/`; `wait` is not an acting RPC. Until step 6 sets a number the budget is unlimited, but every decision is recorded, which is what step 5 measures. This step adds new mail to what `wait` returns on, and its per-caller cursor already keeps mail that arrived mid-turn for the next wait. Every `ao` command's output ends with `[agentorc] you have N unread messages — run ao inbox` while the caller has unread mail, plus `(wake budget spent)` while it is. Neither types into a pane.
4. **The briefs**: a lead's round reads its inbox at the top; the grinder mails its lead when a reference is done instead of relying on the lead noticing, and, until 2026-09-20, sent a `note` to its sibling workers (the same-team edge) when it **claimed** one, which is what let two workers learn the other held a reference before duplicating it. That at-claim note was always a stopgap and is gone: TD-056's reference leases replaced it once the running host agent was seen refusing a second live session's claim by name.
5. **Run it and measure.** A lead and two grinders on steps 1–4 for a few evenings, recording per session the mail-caused wakes (`wait` returning on mail), **separately** the free wakes that carried mail alongside a member's change (the grinder marks `done` and mails its lead at once, so without this split leads would read as needing no budget), exchanges per thread, mailbox depth reached, and how long read entries stay useful.
6. **The bounds, with numbers from step 5**, written into §4.10 in the same PR with the evidence that would change them: the **wake budget** as mail-caused wakes per rolling window, refilled by time and in full by a person's **act** toward the session (a send, a reply, an answer — never opening Focus or reading), never by a controller's send or a non-mail turn; a controller's `send` never charged (the round-two charge was dropped in the fourth review as redundant with the wake charge); exhaustion visible on the record and to the sender; the mailbox depth, refusing with a reason the sender sees; the per-thread exchange bound and the pair window, past which the host agent **refuses the send** naming the bound and the thread and writes a `bound_hit` mark every participant's card and `ao` replies show (the sender writes any board line itself — the host agent never commits to a board here); an `ask`'s default bound and the *expired* mark on both cards and the asker's `ao` replies, including the gone-addressee case; the `text` cap; the delivery damping (identical-repeat window and per-sender rate, both refusing with a reason); the retention window and pruning, open asks exempt; the person inbox's depth, whose refusal names the board.
7. **The doorbell** (§4.10, 2026-09-16): (a) the fixed line — an unread count and nothing a sender wrote, with a test that a crafted `about` never reaches the pane; (b) submitted through `_submit` only on a hook-confirmed `idle` **with an empty composer**, coalesced per idle stretch, spending wake budget, and skipped for a person's session, a session with no `composer()`, a spent budget, a wrap-up under way or `out_of_work`; rung only when the unread count has risen since the last ring; retried once, then recorded. A session that has only declared `out_of_work` may be rung — only a wrap-up or wind-down under way beats mail. No `Stop`-hook block — cut in review; `Stop` stays fire-and-forget.
8. **The UI**: the Inbox panel on Focus beside Reports, the unread chip on the card, Reply, a person's **Message** control, and the person inbox in the Org top bar (§4.5a's five rows).

**Deliberately not in scope:** any broadcast, channel or room; mail as a durable record (the ledger and the board keep that job — invariant 13); and messaging across hosts, which design §4.4a routes through the home host agent and TD-057 builds.

**A further step, only when a second adapter exists:** `mail` on the adapter contract (§4.3) — how a session of a given tool is handed entries and woken by them. Claude Code's answer is `ao inbox` and `ao wait`, which is why steps 1–8 can be built without it; the contract entry earns its keep the moment a tool with no shell, or one whose turns a harness composes, gets an adapter. Until then the honest statement is the one §4.10 makes: an adapter with no native path falls back to a pane write, which is a `send`, and the message/control line is that adapter's convention rather than the host agent's gate. Sequenced with TD-035, which is not itself the work of building a second adapter but the design question that second adapter forces — dev-cadence's SessionStart wiring is Claude Code's — and this step is the same question asked of mail: both are answered by the adapter, and neither can be answered before one exists.

**Done when** a worker tells its lead it finished without the lead polling, and an idle worker is rung for a message with nothing its sender wrote typed into its pane; a worker reaches its person through the person inbox; two leads over one worker settle a contradiction between themselves and land a board line when they cannot; a message to a person's session is delivered where a send is refused; every refusal names §4.10's graph; a session out of wake budget receives mail that lands without waking it, visibly to itself and to the sender; and a person's session is never woken by mail at all.

**Related:** design §4.10 (the whole section), §4.5a, §4.7, §9 invariants 5, 11 and 13, §10 (the 2026-09-14 entry, and the 2026-09-13 one it answers); TD-039 (the conflict half — a `conflict` message and its exchange, which step 6 bounds), TD-049 (the wake, absorbed by step 3), TD-036 (the graph the gate reads), TD-028 (the report channels the Inbox sits beside and deliberately is not).

## TD-053: A team never winds down when it runs out of work — build design §4.9a

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-14 (raised by Paul)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** evaluation
**Status:** Partly done — **the design landed first, as §4.9a (2026-09-14)**, with two rows in §4.5a, a sentence in §4.7, a paragraph on the `progress` channel and a pointer under the role table in §4.8, invariant 14 added, and the §10 question answered. **Step 1 landed 2026-09-17**: `out_of_work: {at, why}` on the record (home-owned, persisted), written by `progress` with `status="none"` — `ao progress none --why` — refused without a reason, refused as `derived`, and refused from anyone but the session itself (invariant 14's *written only by the session it is about*); a later declared claim clears it (a decision this step had to make: a session that claims has work again); it is in the wake digest, so a lead blocked in `ao wait` hears it; `ao status -v` prints it, and the skill file carries the verb. **Step 2 landed 2026-09-17 (PR #179)**: §4.9a's per-role test is in the `grinder`, `hunter` and `orchestrator` templates (`src/agentorc/briefs/`) and in `docs/briefs/tdgrind-ao-1.md` / `orchestrator-ao-1.md` — each says what it must have searched, to declare **before** exiting, and what to do when a pre-build agent refuses `none` (the live one answers `missing 1 required positional argument: 'ref'`, since the new CLI sends no reference — the briefs were corrected to say so); the grinder test adds re-reading the ledger from `origin` first, the cheapest guard against a false empty. The lead's *behaviour* (not nudging or restarting an out-of-work member, the wind-down) was step 3 and was deliberately left out of the briefs by step 2. **Seen live 2026-09-17 (samscrape-grind, the first run with steps 1–2):** all three free-pick grinders merged one item each, then declared `out_of_work` with a `why` naming every open entry and what gates it, and mailed their lead — the declaration works. The team then did not wind down, which is step 3 unbuilt and nothing else: a Claude Code worker's `/exit` does not leave (it sits idle at the prompt), the lead's brief winds down only when members have *exited* and forbids closing an idle member that has no stop time, so the lead logs *out of work, not closing* every ten minutes indefinitely. Step 3 must treat *idle and declared* as finished and let the lead close such a member. **Step 3 landed 2026-09-17 (PR #192):** design §4.9a gained *Finished means declared, not gone* (a member is finished when `out_of_work` is on its record and it is idle, exited or closed) and *The lead runs the stop itself*; `ao team stop` gained `--close` (closes each member that settled with no uncommitted file and no unpushed commit, names any it left open), never types at or kills a lead that is the caller, sends no wrap-up to a finished member, and no longer prints the lead as *still working*; the lead template and `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.md` carry the round rule (a finished member is never sent to or restarted) and the four-step ending (`ao team stop <team> --close`, the declaration, push the log, `ao close` on its own id). **Seen live 2026-09-17 23:16–23:19Z (ao-grind):** `tdgrind-ao-1` declared `out_of_work` after four items; its lead, woken from `ao wait` by the change, ran `ao team stop ao-grind --close --timeout 90` (the member closed 23:18:37Z, nothing left open), recorded its own `ao progress none --why "Every member finished: …"`, pushed its log and ran `ao close` on its own id (23:19:10Z) — three minutes from declaration to a stopped team, nobody involved. The night before, the same state held two teams up for ten hours, by their leads' own count. **Step 6 landed 2026-09-17 (PR #203, session `tdgrind-ao-1`):** both §4.5a rows. The **out of work** chip is on the card beside the report line and in the Focus header — the words fixed, the `why` on hover (a reason names every entry the session looked at and what gates each, which a card cannot hold), drawn for any session that declared and gone the moment it claims again, which is the record's own rule. It is not a state: the card still reads `idle`. The report row is now drawn for a declaration even when neither channel has anything in it. The **Teams** strip reads *wound down <t> ago* rather than *stopped* when nothing carrying the badge is live and every session that carried it declared — all or nothing, from the records rather than from any count of ledger rows, so a team one member never declared on reads *stopped*, and a definition nothing has ever carried is neither. `ao team list` prints the same word from the same rows, so the page and the CLI cannot disagree about one definition. Note which state the strip surfaces: a worker that declared but has not been stopped still sits `idle`, so its team is live and its group card is the surface until step 3's `ao team stop --close` closes it — the strip is the *after*. The review of that PR found the one thing worth finding: both readers treated `out_of_work` as a `{at, why}` dict on sight, and `view` runs for every session on the grid — so a record left in any other shape by a different build, a bug or a hand repair would have taken down the whole Org page rather than costing one card its chip, which is PR #131's `run_until` *half six* failure again in a new field. Both readers check the shape now, `_age` returns "" for anything it cannot read rather than raising, and the tests feed both a string, a list, a number and three malformed instants. **Step 5 built 2026-09-20 (PR #340, `grinder-ao-1`)** in the package's template, `src/agentorc/briefs/manager.md`: after `ao progress none`, one line on the board, `Due:` today, saying the team ran out of work at `<t>` and what each member looked for, from each record's `why`, before the log is pushed and the manager closes itself. **`docs/briefs/manager-ao-1.md`, the repo's own manager brief, still lacks it**; `docs/briefs/` is the anchor's (its instruction to this team, 2026-09-21; the briefs were renamed by it under TD-076 step 4a), and a brief takes effect at a team start. Step 4 is open: the three guard numbers, chosen against a running fleet. **Next:** step 4's numbers need a running fleet; the brief line is in `docs/briefs/`.

**Location:** `src/sessionorc/models.py` (`out_of_work` on the record), `src/sessionorc/agent.py` (the `progress` RPC's new entry shape), `src/agentorc/cli.py` (`ao progress none`), `src/agentorc/teamrun.py` (the wind-down calls the existing stop sequence), `src/agentorc/ui/` (the chip and the strip note), `agentorc/briefs/*.md` (each role's test), `docs/briefs/orchestrator-ao-1.md`, design §4.9a / §4.5a / §4.7 / §4.8 / §9 invariant 14

**Why:** every stopper in §6 is a clock or a cap — stop time, run window, usage gate, credential lapse, stall — and none of them answers *is there anything left to do?*; `ao team stop` is a person's command that no condition calls (`teamrun.stop_members` runs only when a caller calls it). What looked like wind-down was three brief paragraphs agreeing with each other: the worker's *stop when your lane is done*, the orchestrator's restart rule keyed on *lane items still open*, and the orchestrator's *stop when every member has exited*. That cascade holds for a fixed lane and breaks for `free-pick`, which is the lane the ao-grind team actually runs: *lane done* never becomes true, the lead's idle rule (idle **with lane items not done**) never matches a worker with no lane items, so an idle free-pick worker matches no rule at all and nothing notices it — and the lead cannot end after members that cannot end. Paul, 2026-09-14: *"Does our design also have a team wind down if it runs out of work to do? e.g. no production system to keep checking, no TDs?"* Those two examples sit either side of the distinction §4.9a turns on: a role that consumes a list ends when the list ends; a role that watches a stream is *waiting* when its source goes quiet, and waiting is not finishing.

**Fix, in order — each step is usable on its own:**

1. **The declaration.** `out_of_work: {at, why}` as its own field on the session record — beside the `progress` entry list, never an entry in it, so the upsert-by-reference rule is untouched — written only by `ao progress none --why` on the ungated `progress` channel, persisted and reloaded with the record, never derived (§9 invariant 14). A `why` is required: a declaration without one is refused, naming the rule. Tests: the field survives a reload; a derived-report tick never writes it; `ao status -v` and `--json` show it.
2. **The role tests, in the briefs.** §4.9a's table written into each `agentorc/briefs/<role>.md` template: what a fixed-lane grinder, a free-pick grinder, a hunter and an orchestrator each have to have searched before they may declare it, and the rule that they declare it *before* exiting rather than just exiting. This is prose, and it is the step that makes the rest mean anything.
3. **The lead's half.** The orchestrator's tick: an out-of-work member is not nudged and not restarted; a member that exited **without** declaring it is still a crash and is still restarted (that split is the whole value of step 1); and when every member is out of work or exited having said so, the lead runs the wind-down. The wind-down is `teamrun`'s existing stop sequence called with a different trigger — wrap up the members, wait, then the lead — never a second code path.
4. **The three guards, whose numbers §4.9a deliberately leaves unset:** the lead re-reads the ledger itself before accepting a **team-wide** wind-down (one cheap second opinion catches every mechanical false negative — a `gh` outage, a moved ledger file, a path that changed under a grep); an exhaustion declared within *n* minutes of a session's start is reported rather than acted on; and a member's own re-check interval before it may declare. Choose the numbers here against a running fleet, write them into the design in the same PR, and say what evidence would change them. The failure to guard against is not a team that runs on too long — it is one that stands down because it looked wrong.
5. **The board line.** The lead's last act before its own exit: one line on `docs/user_attention.md` naming the time and, per member, what it looked for and did not find, taken from each record's `why`. This is the point of the mechanism — the fleet has finished the work a person defined, and the next move is a person's; a team that dissolves quietly is harder to notice than one that says so.
6. **The surfaces** (§4.5a's two rows): the **out of work** chip on the card and Focus header with the `why` on hover, and the Teams strip reading *wound down <t>* rather than a bare zero live count.

**Deliberately not in scope:** restarting a team when work reappears — a fleet that starts itself is a scheduler, which is TD-026's question and a different one; and any core-side count of open ledger rows, which §4.9a rejects by name.

**Related:** design §4.9a (the whole section), §4.9 (the stop sequence this reuses, and `ao team start` already being the restart), §4.8 (the `progress` channel and the role presets that carry the tests), §6 (the clock-and-cap stoppers this is orthogonal to), §9 invariant 14, §10 (the 2026-09-14 entry); TD-032 (a stood-down worker sitting `idle` for 20 h with nothing noticing — the same blind spot from the other side); TD-042 (a brief describes the job, not the run — why the test lives in a repeatable template); TD-052 (mail is how *there is work now* reaches an out-of-work session that is still alive, §4.10's wake budget); TD-026 (the schedule that would start a wound-down team again).

## TD-055: Rename to the glossary's decided words — `lead`, `director`, the `control` grant, `round`, and the retired synonyms

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-16 (raised by Paul)
**Owner:** paul
**Kind:** build
**Status:** Partly done — the words were decided 2026-09-16 and recorded in `docs/glossary.md` (widened the same day after a Fable review of the whole vocabulary). **Step 1 landed 2026-09-17** (docs and briefs, text only): live `docs/design.md`, `docs/briefs/`, `src/agentorc/briefs/*.md` and `src/agentorc/skill.md` say *lead*, *director* (`docs/briefs/orc-of-orcs.md` → `director.md`), *the host agent*, a lead's *round*, *send*, *policies*/*lead* and *org*/*command session*. Deliberately left for steps 2 and 3, because they are code values the text must keep matching until the code changes: the role value `orchestrator` (`role: orchestrator`, `briefs/orchestrator.md`), the grant `orchestrate`, and `<orc>` in `ao control <orc>` and the card's *under `<orc>`* chip. Also left as written: the UI label **show command runs** (a code string; the §4.5a row must match the control), live session and brief names (step 4), the historical `tdgrind` supervisor script, `nudge_user_attention.py`, prior-art product descriptions in §3, and every dated record (§10, `docs/decisions/`, the archive). **Step 2 landed 2026-09-17**: the preset is `lead` (`repoconfig.PRESETS`, `agentorc/briefs/lead.md`, `org.DEFAULT_LEAD_ROLE`), and `repoconfig.ROLE_ALIASES` keeps `orchestrator` resolving for one release — as `--role`, a team's `role:`, and a `roles:` key in either config layer — with one stderr line per process; a team start records the new name even from an old definition. Design §4.5a/§4.8/§4.9/§4.9a/§5 and the glossary say `lead`; source comments that used *orchestrator* for the position were reworded. Paul's live `~/.agentorc/org.yml` still says `role: orchestrator` and a `roles: orchestrator:` overlay: it keeps working through the alias and prints the line — renaming those keys is his (board). **Step 3 landed 2026-09-17**: the grant is `control` (`models.GRANTS`), with `models.GRANT_ALIASES` reading `orchestrate` for one release — a stored record is normalised on load and rewritten (the host agent logs it), `set_grants` / `create` accept it, `--grant` and `ao grant`/`ao revoke` accept it and print a line, and a `grants:` in `.agentorc.yml` or an `org.yml` team says so once; the gate and the UI check `has_control`, which reads either name, so a UI or CLI newer than the running host agent still recognises its records. `ao control <controller> add|remove`, the card's *under `<controller>`* chip, the no-controller hint, the UI's Grants checkbox and notes, design (§4.5a, §4.7, §4.8, §4.9, §4.10, §5, §9), the skill file and every brief say `control`. **Skew, until the host agent restarts:** a `control` sent by the new client is refused by an agent still running the old code (`unknown grant control`) — `ao team start` of a team with a lead, `ao new --role lead`, `ao grant … control`; restart the host agent before those (board). Step 4 (live session and brief names) is Paul's and not done. ~~**What remains after the alias release:** delete `ROLE_ALIASES` and `GRANT_ALIASES` and their tests.~~ **Done by TD-107 (2026-09-22, PRs #440 and #442)**, which removed the aliases outright rather than after a release — so the *done when* below that asks old words to keep working with a deprecation line is superseded: they are unknown words now (design §4.8 *Role names*). Step 4 is done for the teams that run (TD-076 step 4a, 2026-09-20); **what is left of this entry is the one brief never launched, `docs/briefs/guardians-orchestrator.md`** — its name and its words (it still says *lead* for the manager), Paul's to change when the guardians team is first stood up. **The mockups were renamed 2026-09-20** with TD-074's step 6 redraw: the artboards say `lead` and `control`, in the role badges, the Focus headers and the notes. The session *names* they show (`orchestrator-ao-1`) are step 4's and stay as they are. **Next:** what is left is `docs/briefs/guardians-orchestrator.md`, Paul's when that team stands up.

**Location:** `src/agentorc/repoconfig.py` (the built-in `orchestrator` role, the commented example), `src/agentorc/org.py` (`DEFAULT_LEAD_ROLE = "orchestrator"` and its example), `src/agentorc/briefs/orchestrator.md`, `src/agentorc/teamrun.py`, `src/agentorc/cli.py` (`ao control`'s positional `orc` and its help text, the `<orc>` in `cmd_control`'s docstring and in the no-controller hint at `cli.py:478`, `--grant`), `src/sessionorc/models.py` (`GRANTS`) and `src/sessionorc/agent.py` (`rpc_set_grants`, `_gate`), `src/agentorc/skill.md`, `src/agentorc/ui/` (the Grants checkbox, the "under `<orc>`" wording, any user-facing "orchestrator"), `tests/`, `docs/design.md` (every live use — not §10 or other dated history), `docs/briefs/`, `CLAUDE.md`

**Why:** "orchestrator" meant a team lead in about 200 places while the top coordinator was "orc-of-orcs"; bare "the agent" meant the host daemon in about 70 places of a design whose ADR calls an AI session an *agent*; *tick* meant both the host agent's loop (in code) and a lead's loop (in briefs); and *nudge*, *supervisor* and *fleet* each gave one thing a second name. Paul, 2026-09-16: *"having the names nailed down will reduce confusion"* and *"make the names as intuitive as possible, and keep a single genre when appropriate."* The glossary's rule: organisation words are workplace words, mechanism words are plain and tool-native, no third genre (doorbell the named exception).

**Fix, in order — each its own PR:**

1. **Docs and briefs** (text only). In `docs/design.md` live text, `docs/briefs/`, `src/agentorc/briefs/*.md`, `src/agentorc/skill.md` and `CLAUDE.md`: *orchestrator* as a position → *lead*; *orc-of-orcs* → *director* (`docs/briefs/orc-of-orcs.md` → `director.md`); bare *the agent* for the daemon → *the host agent*; a lead's *tick* → *round*, while the host agent's stays *tick* (including §4.10's "the agent's next pass" → "the host agent's next tick"); *nudge* → *send*; *supervisor* → *lead*, or *policies* where §6's mechanism is meant (the historical "tdgrind supervisor" script keeps its name); *fleet* → *org* or "every live session"; *command run* as a noun → *command session*; "run N" never written; the lead brief's "your reach is your members" → "your members are …"; §5's `name: orc` example → `name: lead`. Dated history (§10 entries, `docs/decisions/`, the archive, old board lines) is left as written. **Already done 2026-09-16:** §4.10, the §4.5a mail rows, invariants 5, 11 and 13 (invariant 9 checked and needed nothing), and TD-052 (the mail design worked on that day).
2. **The role preset.** `orchestrator` → `lead` in `repoconfig.py`'s built-ins, the brief template file, tests, and any `.agentorc.yml` or team definition that names `role: orchestrator`. Accept `orchestrator` as a deprecated alias for one release, printing a line that names the new word.
3. **The grant.** `orchestrate` → `control` in the grant list, the gate's check, `--grant`, role `grants:`, the UI checkbox and `ao --skill`. Records and configs already carrying `orchestrate` are read as `control` for one release (normalised on load, so the record is rewritten with the new value), with a deprecation line. CLI help and hints stop using `<orc>`: `ao control <controller> add|remove …`.
4. **Live session and brief names** (`orchestrator-ao-1`, `guardians-orchestrator`) — Paul's call, since renaming a live session changes what its members' `controllers` name; do it at the next planned restart, not mid-run.

**Not in scope:** record field names (`controllers`, `capabilities`, `role`, `lane`, …), state names, `ao` verbs, package and command names, and code variable names such as `fleet` — the glossary keeps all of them deliberately.

**Done when** no live text in `docs/design.md`, `src/` or the briefs uses *orchestrator* for a position, bare *agent* for the daemon, *tick* for a lead's loop, or *nudge*/*supervisor*/*fleet* in prose; `ao new --role lead` and `ao grant <id> control` work; and `--role orchestrator`, `orchestrate` in a config and `orchestrate` on an existing record still work with their deprecation line.

**Related:** `docs/glossary.md`; ADR `docs/decisions/2026-09-13-org-teams-projects.md` (the Org/Team/Project/Role vocabulary this extends); design §4.8 (role presets, grants, *Orc-of-orcs is not a special case*), §4.9 (team definitions), §6 (policies), §9 invariants 1, 9 and 11.

## TD-060: The name `agentorc` is taken — rename the project

**Priority:** Medium
**Added:** 2026-09-16 (Paul; discussed in samscrape session 746e1973 on kmaster)
**Owner:** paul
**Kind:** build
**Status:** **Paul, 2026-10-07:** snoozed to 2026-10-21. **Owner paul since 2026-10-06 (the anchor):** nothing in it is a member's to pick. Step 2 waits for the release boundary Paul picks. Open — **Paul, 2026-10-04: the anchor picks the quiet window for step 2 and runs it** (no team running, both units stopped). **decided by Paul 2026-09-22: go with the rename to `shiftlead`** (his words: *go with the rename to shiftlead, we will have to figure out when to rename the repo*). **Step 1 is done:** its remaining half, the visible name — the UI wordmark in `base.html` (*Shift**Lead***), every page's `<title>` (*Org · ShiftLead*) and the mockups' wordmark — landed 2026-09-22 (grinder-ao-2). **Step 2, the machine names** (`~/.agentorc`, the seven env vars, the units, the venv, `.agentorc.yml`, the `agentorc-hook` entry point, with the migration and its test), waits for a release boundary with no team running and both units stopped, as *Fix* says, and the anchor runs it. **When the GitHub repo is renamed is Paul's, and open** (board line, 2026-09-22): it can follow step 2 or go first, since GitHub redirects the old name. Was: awaiting Paul's decision on the name. `shiftlead` is the leading candidate (free on PyPI, npm, crates.io and as a GitHub org when checked 2026-09-16; `.dev`/`.org`/`.tech` open per Paul); Paul asked for time to think. Nothing is renamed. **Both names were claimed 2026-09-17 ahead of the decision:** PyPI `shiftlead 0.0.0` (empty placeholder) and the GitHub org `ShiftLead`; the domains are not registered. The candidates, the test they were held to, the measured surface and the six decisions are in [ADR 2026-09-16](decisions/2026-09-16-rename.md). Worktree `agentorc_rename` exists with no commits. **Next:** step 2 waits for the release boundary Paul picks.

**Location:** the whole repo — 1,110 case-insensitive occurrences of `agentorc` in 93 tracked files and 260 of `sessionorc` in 56 (`origin/main` at `8bd21b9`) — plus what lives outside it: `~/.agentorc/`, seven `AGENTORC_*` environment variables (the six below plus `AGENTORC_BUILD`, added since this was measured), `agentorc-agent.service` / `agentorc-ui.service`, `~/.local/share/agentorc-venv`, `.agentorc.yml` in other repos, the `ao-` session-id prefix, and three synced files in dev-cadence (and so in samscrape and contractmatch).

**Why:** another project holds the name (TD-059 is to identify it). This is the second rename (`sessionherd` → `agentorc`, 2026-09-04, chosen to sit beside `cmdorc`), and it is cheaper now than it will ever be again: one user, nothing published to PyPI, no external adapter known. Every week adds briefs, ADRs and session records that carry the old name.

**Fix:** decide the six questions in the ADR (the name; what happens to `sessionorc`; whether the `ao-` id prefix stays as an opaque historical prefix — recommended; whether `ao` survives as an alias; a compatibility window or a clean cut with a `migrate` command; whether to claim the names before deciding). Then, in order: claim PyPI + the GitHub org; land TD-055 first, since it edits many of the same lines; one PR for packages, entry points, env vars, state dir, units and config file name, with the migration and a test for it; the text sweep, **leaving dated records — ADRs, the ledger archive, memories — as written**; dev-cadence's three synced files and a sync; the repo rename and a README tagline drawn from TD-059. **Land the machine-facing step with no team running and both units stopped:** running sessions hold `AGENTORC_SESSION` in their environment and resolve `agentorc-hook` from the unit's PATH, so a rename under a live team breaks every started session's hooks.

**See TD-076 before deciding (2026-09-20):** on 2026-09-19 Paul decided the coordinating role becomes `manager`, the go-between `techlead`, and bare `lead` is retired — which weakens the *shift lead is the job* argument for `shiftlead` and should be weighed in the same sitting.

**2026-09-21, Paul: the spelling is `ShiftLead`, `shiftlead.dev` is bought (Cloudflare), and the command stays `ao`** — short, in every brief and every worker's habit, and `sl` is a joke command on many Linux boxes; `shiftlead` may be a long-form alias. **The order confirmed by Paul 2026-09-21** (*that order works*) — **step 1 begun the same evening: the README's and the design's titles and the glossary say ShiftLead (this PR); the wordmark in `base.html` and the page `<title>` are a worker's, one line each; step 2 waits for a release boundary.** As suggested: **the visible name first** (the UI wordmark, the README, the design's title and this ledger's head — one PR, cheap, the page half a worker's) and **the machine names at a release boundary** (`~/.agentorc`, the seven env vars, the units, the venv, `.agentorc.yml`, with the migration and its test) with no team running, as *Fix* says. **Decided by Paul, 2026-09-20: the project keeps `shiftlead`** — with the roles `manager` and `techlead` (TD-076) — so the name stands for what the product is for, not for a role in it; the ADR's *the metaphor is the job* paragraph is to be reworded when the rename is carried out. What remains of this entry is the carrying out.

**Done when** the installed distribution, both units, the state directory and the README carry the new name; a session started before the rename is either migrated or documented as needing a restart; `git grep -i agentorc` returns only dated records and the "was `agentorc`" lines; and the three dev-cadence files are synced.

**Related:** TD-059 (the neighbours the name search found; its survey supplies the tagline), TD-055 (the glossary rename — `lead` is already the decided word, which is much of why `shiftlead` fits), design §4.1 (session ids), §4.3 (the two packages). **README positioning (TD-059, 2026-09-20):** the first-screen sentence drawn from the neighbours survey is in [ADR 2026-09-20](decisions/2026-09-20-session-desk-neighbours.md) (c); it goes in with the rename.

## TD-063: Three CI-only flakes on the 3.12 runner — two failures and a hang

**Priority:** Low
**Added:** 2026-09-17 (session `tdgrind-ao-1`, both on PRs whose diffs did not touch the code under test)
**Owner:** anchor
**Kind:** evaluation
**Status:** Partly done — all three named tests are fixed and PR #192's `-vv` proved itself twice over; the PR #192 hang itself is still unattributed. **A hang now fails with a stack, 2026-09-20 (PR #314, `tdgrind-ao-1`):** `pytest-timeout` with `timeout = 120` and `timeout_method = "thread"`, so the next one is *evidence* instead of silence. It was earned by a second sighting the same day — `tdgrind-ao-2`'s run 35548953938, where 3.13 hung for 14 minutes on a branch touching only `src/agentorc/ui` and two docs; the job's last line was `test_limited_from_one_daily_window PASSED`, and the next test in file order is `test_send_wait_three_outcomes`, the one TD-078 names. That is all a hang leaves: the test *before* it, and a cancel. With the bound it stops in the right file, at the line that is waiting, with **every thread's stack** — which is the only thing that can attribute a wait with no bound. Say what `thread` costs, since it is not an ordinary failure: on expiry it dumps the stacks and `os._exit`s, so the run ends there, with no fixture teardown and the remaining tests unrun. That is the right trade here and `signal` is not, because the hangs this is aimed at are in *threads* — `asyncio.to_thread`, a tmux read, a client waiting on a reply — and a signal can only raise in the main thread: it would fail the test and leave the stuck thread running. A stopped run with stacks beats a cancelled job with none. The bound is per **test item**, setup to teardown, at a tenfold margin over the slowest test here (about 10 s), and it was proved on a deliberate `sleep(300)`, which stops at the line. **The hang itself is still unattributed**, and the next sighting is what will settle it. **What the `-vv` bought (2026-09-17, PR #197):** both 3.12 jobs of that PR hung for the full 15 minutes and the per-test line named the test in one look — a *new* test of that PR, whose `async with server` ends in `asyncio.Server.wait_closed()`. On 3.12 `Server.close()` does not cancel the tasks running the handlers and `wait_closed()` waits for them, so a handler still in `readline()` hangs the run; 3.13 cancels them and passed the same commit in 1m30s twice. Fixed in that PR by tearing the server down by hand. It is **not** the PR #192 hang — that test did not exist then — but it is the same shape, and it is the reason to suspect an `asyncio` teardown rather than tmux for the earlier one. **A fourth flake, found the same way (PR #197, run 35277257855, test (3.12), 1m39):** `test_agent.py::test_derived_entries_go_to_the_record_that_holds_the_directory` failed with `ao-repo-run-1 is idle; kill it first` on its own `remove` — a record it had killed and seen go `exited` was `idle` again. This one is a **bug in the host agent, not in the test**, and is fixed below. **Next:** the PR #192 hang waits for a stack from `pytest-timeout`.

**A pane list older than a kill revived the record it named.** `tick()` reads the pane list in one `to_thread` and the tails in another, then reconciles — and an RPC runs on the loop inside that window. A `kill` there left the tick holding a list that still named the session: `_observe` set `pane` back to True and read a state off the last screen, so the `exited` record came back as `idle`, and `remove` then refused it with *kill it first*. On a fast machine the window is invisible; a loaded 3.12 runner found it. The fix is a `_killed_at` stamp taken by `rpc_kill` and checked in `_reconcile`: a snapshot older than the kill is not allowed to observe that record, and the stamp is dropped as soon as a newer snapshot arrives, so it holds at most one tick's worth of ids. `rpc_close` needs no stamp — a `closed` record is skipped before its pane is ever observed — but it *clears* one, because a `kill` then a `close` inside one tick would otherwise strand a stamp for `CLOSED_KEEP`, which no reconcile would ever reach (review of PR #199). `test_a_pane_list_taken_before_a_kill_does_not_revive_the_record` hands `_reconcile` exactly the snapshot the in-flight tick would have had; without the guard it reports `idle != exited`, which is the CI failure verbatim.

**And why a hang had no evidence at all, 2026-09-21 (found by `tdgrind-ao-2`, built in PR #315):** `LocalClient.call` had **no bound**. Every bound in a test is on the test's own side — `wait_state` at 6 s, each `asyncio.wait_for` at 5 — so a test *cannot* hang inside its own assertions; it can only hang in `await self._reader.readline()`, waiting for a reply that is never coming. That is why the two sightings named a neighbour rather than a line.
